metrics = {'T_median': 22.633204633204635, 'T_median_ok': False, 'T_median_ratio': 0.7291911532875389, 'contaminated_files': {}, ...}
submitted_data_stats = {'T_max': 178, 'T_mean': 22.250439242927023, 'T_median': 22.633204633204635, 'T_min': 11, ...}
reference_data_stats = {'chance_majority': {'licking': 0.9588318123909042, 'position': 0.25723790510097916, 'running_speed': 0.25029903335979...y': {'T_max': 238, 'T_mean': 32.62721547677432, 'T_median': 31.0387811634349, 'T_min': 11, ...}, 'device': 'cuda', ...}

    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Check the converted dataset matches the reference in size, variables and ranges.
    
        Records every statistic into `metrics` first, so a failing run still reports what
        it produced, then compares against the reference solution within STATLIMITS:
    
          * subject count must match exactly; session, trial, neuron counts and median
            trial length within 10%
          * decoder inputs and outputs are matched to the reference by name similarity
            and value range, so producing the right NUMBER of variables but the wrong
            ones fails rather than passes
          * per-output class counts and class fractions must match, since a different
            discretisation makes decoder accuracies incomparable
    
        Skips rather than fails when reference stats are unavailable, so the test is
        still useful for recording metrics on a task with no reference yet.
        """
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi))+1
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
            # Verdict recorded where the threshold is applied; the assert below reads it back.
            # The assert loop bails on its first failure, so computing these here is what makes
            # a later field's result knowable after an earlier one has failed.
            metrics[field+'_ok'] = bool(
                ratio_within_limits(metrics[field+'_ratio'], STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']))
    
        # Seed a per-variable verdict for every REFERENCE output, so a variable the agent never
        # produced -- and which therefore never appears in output_matches below -- counts as a
        # miss rather than shrinking the denominator of the fraction categories.
        reference_output_names = list(reference_data_stats['data_summary'].get('output_range') or [])
        for ref_name in reference_output_names:
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = False
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        # Named for what it is: the mean Hungarian MATCHING cost, which is dominated
        # by name similarity and is forced to 0 for exactly-matching names before any
        # range term is computed. It is not a mean of the input_range_error_<var>
        # fields -- that is mean_input_range_error, recorded below.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_match_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            scale = abs(ref_hi - ref_lo)
            if scale < 1e-6 and abs(sub_hi - sub_lo) < 1e-6:
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = 0.0
            else:
                scale = max(scale, 1e-6)
                metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo)/scale, abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)/scale))
        mean_input_range_error = np.mean([metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] for ref_name, _, _ in input_matches]) if len(input_matches) else 0.0
        metrics['mean_input_range_error'] = mean_input_range_error
        # Recorded ONLY when there is something to match. A task with no decoder inputs
        # (allen2p: dinput == 0, input_range == {}) has no input ranges to compare, so leaving
        # the key unset scores the category undefined and drops it from the mean -- rather than
        # awarding a free 1.0 for a check that never happened. Matches undefined_categories()
        # in lesion_analysis.py, which singles out this same case. The assert below is guarded
        # by the same condition, so it never reads a key that was not written.
        if len(input_matches):
            metrics['input_range_ok'] = bool(
                mean_input_range_error <= STATLIMITS['input_range_error'])
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]
    
        # As above: the mean matching cost, not a mean of output_fraction_error_<var>.
        mean_output_match_cost = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
        metrics['output_match_mean_cost'] = mean_output_match_cost
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(abs(sub_hi - ref_hi))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi) + 1)
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi) + 1)
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())
            if len(ref_fracs) == len(sub_fracs):
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None
    
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] <= STATLIMITS['output_range_error'])
            # A None error means the class counts differ, so there is no comparable fraction
            # vector. That scores as a miss, but is NOT asserted on below -- the nclasses check
            # already fails that case, and asserting here too would change what the suite
            # rejects. Verdict and assert diverge only here, deliberately.
            frac_error = metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}']
            metrics[f'output_fraction_ok_{ref_name}'] = bool(
                frac_error is not None and frac_error <= STATLIMITS['output_fraction_error'])
    
        for field in fields_check:
>           assert metrics[field+'_ok'], f"{field} in submitted data ({submitted_data_stats[field]}) is not within {STATLIMITS[field+'_ratio']*100}% of reference ({reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]})"
E           AssertionError: T_median in submitted data (22.633204633204635) is not within 10.0% of reference (31.0387811634349)
E           assert False

They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   105→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   106→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   107→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   108→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   109→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   110→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   111→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   112→\n   113→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   114→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   115→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   116→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   117→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   118→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   119→\n   120→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   121→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   122→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   123→\n   124→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   125→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   126→\n   127→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   128→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   129→\n   130→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   131→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   132→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   133→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   134→\n   135→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   136→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   137→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   138→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   139→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   140→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   141→\n   142→## Decoder Reference\n   143→\n   144→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   145→- Optional arguments:\n   146→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   147→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   148→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   149→\n   150→### Purpose\n   151→\n   152→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   153→1. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"Unsupervised pretraining in biological neural networks\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally aligned based on trial start (corridor entry).\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time to sound cue, continuous, time-varying\n- Day of training, continuous, per-trial\n- Time since trial start, continuous, time varying\n- Reward availability: 1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not, discrete, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Visual stimulus category. e.g. circle, leaf, etc., per-trial\n- Licking, binary, time-varying. 0 = not licking, 1 = licking\n- Position in corridor discretized into 4 equal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins, time varying\n- Running speed discretized into 4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n    54→\n    55→ii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n    67→\n    68→## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n    69→\n    70→i. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n    71→\n    72→ii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n    73→```python\n    74→trials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n    75→          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n    76→```\n    77→```python\n    78→lengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\n    79→return np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n    80→```\n    81→\n    82→iii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n    83→\n    84→## 2-a. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n   138→\n   139→## 2-e. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Everything is read from three directories under `data`: `beh` for the behavior, `spk` for the deconvolved calcium traces and `retinotopy` for the visual area of each neuron. `beh/Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is the master index and is read first, listing every recording grouped by experiment type. A recording is one mouse on one date in one block, and that triple is the session id, which also names the spike file. Each `Beh_<exp_type>.npy` holds the behavior of several sessions keyed by that id, so the conversion loops over behavior files and reads each one once; the spikes and the retinotopy are read per session.\n\nii. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The split into trials is given by the data: every neural frame carries its trial in `ft_trInd`, and the boundaries are listed as frame numbers as well, `StartFr` at corridor entry, `GrayFr` at the grey space and `EndFr` at the exit. Based on the instruction we define the trials and the sequence between entry to the end of texture (4m). The trials are taken as they are, and within each one the frames kept are every frame inside the texture, `ft_CorrSpc`. Nothing is cut from a trial: it runs to the end of its own traversal, so trials differ in length.\n\nii. The trials are the ones the data declares, `ntrials` of them, indexed by `ft_trInd`:\n```python\nframes = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n```\n\nand the frames of each are the ones it labels with that trial:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n\niii. Trial goes from start to either gray or end frame, as described in the paper. As neural decoders generally make use of temporally contiguous data, we do not drop frames in the middle of trials. \n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two trials are dropped: one left with no frames once the behavior is cut to the frames that were imaged, and one longer than the 99th percentile of traversals across the whole dataset. Nothing else is filtered.\n\nii. The two conditions, and the limit they use:\n```python\ntrials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n          if 0 < frames[trial].size <= limit]\n```\n```python\nlengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\nreturn np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n```\n\niii. The longest traversals are not slow runs, they are animals that stopped. A trial past the 99th percentile is 95% stationary, against 4% for a typical trial, so the distribution separates rather than shading off. The extreme case, trial 391 of `TX88_2022_07_19_1`, runs 5607 frames: the mouse enters, reaches 1.4 m of the 4 m corridor, stands there for 29 minutes and then runs out, contributing 5571 bins that all carry the same position label. Dropping such a trial whole is preferred to shortening it, which would keep a few hundred bins of a parked animal and invent an endpoint the traversal never had. The threshold is measured from the data rather than written down, over every session even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample run and a full one agree about which trials are too long; it comes out at 238.9 frames, 75 s, about ten times the median traversal, and removes 382 trials of 38110. No session loses more than 9.5% of its trials and none falls under the two needed to evaluate a decoder.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. From `spks` in `spk/<session_id>_neural_data.npy`, which is a list of one neurons by frames array per imaging plane, concatenated into a single array. The neural data is read directly, nothing is derived from it.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The traces are not processed. The file already holds deconvolved traces, which the methods state every analysis was based on, so no dF/F or deconvolution is needed. float16 rather than float32 because the dataset is quite big. \n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The only filter is the visual area. A neuron is kept if its `iarea` falls in V1, mHV, lHV or aHV, and dropped otherwise, which keeps 4,105,393 of 4,691,034 neurons.\n\nii. The area of each neuron, and the neurons kept:\n```python\nregion = np.full(area.size, -1)\nfor index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n    region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\nkeep = region >= 0\nreturn spikes[keep], region[keep]\n```\n\niii. The authors already curated the neurons with the Suite2p cell classifier, and the reference code applies no further filter, so nothing is done beyond assigning the area.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. The alignment event is corridor entry. Trials are of variable length, because mice run at different speeds, and they are stored at that length: every trial starts at its own corridor entry and ends where its traversal ends. Nothing is cut and nothing is padded.\n\nii. Every frame of the traversal, from corridor entry, and the trial built from exactly those frames:\n```python\ninside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\nreturn np.flatnonzero(inside)\n```\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n```\n\niii. The format does not need one length for every trial. It asks that the bin *size* be the same across trials and sessions, which it is at 315 ms, and it accepts `None` for `off_end` when the trials share no common end. The decoder reads each trial's length off its own array and treats every bin as an independent sample, so a shared window buys nothing. A fixed window costs a great deal: at 32 frames it discarded 435407 real frames, 31.7% of everything inside a corridor, and replaced them with 281757 padding bins, 23.1% of what was written. Trials keep their own length instead, and the small number that are too long to be a traversal at all are dropped whole, as described in 1-e.\n\n## 2-e. The imaging frame is the finest resolution the data has, and every behavior stream is already on the same grid, so there is nothing to resample.\n\n## 3-a. The name of every trial, mapped to its code:\n```python\nstimulus = np.array([STIMULUS.index(TEXTURE[name]) for name in beh['WallName']])\n```\n\niii. Note that `TrialStim` names the stimulus of each trial as well, but in the swap sessions it is masked.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *visual_stimulus*?\n\ni. The 15 names that appear across the dataset are mapped to their base texture through a hard coded table, giving four categories, and the texture is stored as its index into `STIMULUS`. The value is per trial, so it is broadcast across all of that trial's bins.\n\nii. The map and the categories:\n```python\nTEXTURE = {'circle1': 'circle', 'circle2': 'circle', 'circle3': 'circle',\n           'leaf1': 'leaf', 'leaf2': 'leaf', 'leaf3': 'leaf',\n           'leaf1_swap1': 'leaf', 'leaf1_swap2': 'leaf',\n           'rock1': 'rock', 'rock2': 'rock',\n           'wood1': 'wood', 'wood2': 'wood', 'wood5': 'wood',\n           'wood1_swap1': 'wood', 'wood1_swap2': 'wood'}\n\nSTIMULUS = ['circle', 'leaf', 'rock', 'wood']\n```\n```python\nnp.full(n_bins, stimulus[trial])\n```\n\niii. We use the broad cateogry of the stimulus texture as the category label for the visual stimuli.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *licking* derived from?\n\ni. From `LickFr`, the neural frame number of every lick in the session.\n\nii. The lick frames, turned into a flag per frame:\n```python\nlick_frame = beh['LickFr'].astype(int)\nlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n```\n\niii. The licking is directly aviliable from `LickFr`.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *licking*?\n\ni. A frame is 1 if at least one lick falls in it and 0 otherwise. The frame number of a lick is fractional, so it is truncated to the frame it lands in.\n\nii. The flag per frame, and the values of one trial:\n```python\nlicking = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=int)\nlick_frame = beh['LickFr'].astype(int)\nlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n```\n```python\nLICKING = ['no lick', 'lick']\nlicking[window]\n```\n\niii. Simply convert from the from lick_frame to a categorical variable.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `LickFr` indexes the imaging frames, so the flag is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\nlicking[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *position* derived from?\n\ni. From `ft_Pos`, the position inside the corridor at each imaging frame, in decimeters: 0 to 40 across the texture and on to 60 through the grey space.\n\nii. The position of every frame, binned:\n```python\nposition = np.clip(beh['ft_Pos'][:n_frames] // 10, 0, 3).astype(int)\n```\n\niii. Simply convert from decimeters to meters, and split into 4 categories.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *position*?\n\ni. The position is divided by 10 decimeters, which gives the four 1 m bins the task asks for, and stored as an index into `POSITION`.\n\nii. The bins and the values of one trial:\n```python\nPOSITION = ['0-1 m', '1-2 m', '2-3 m', '3-4 m']\n```\n```python\nposition = np.clip(beh['ft_Pos'][:n_frames] // 10, 0, 3).astype(int)\nposition[window]\n```\n\niii. The frames of a trial are only those inside the texture, where the position stays below 40.\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_Pos` gives one position per imaging frame, so it is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\nposition[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *running_speed* derived from?\n\ni. From `ft_RunSpeed`, the running speed of the mouse at each imaging frame.\n\nii. The speed of the frames the dataset keeps:\n```python\nspeed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\n```\n\niii. Directly use running speed and discretize into categories.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *running_speed*?\n\ni. The speeds are split into four bins holding a quarter of the frames each. The split is on rank rather than on a threshold, and is taken over the frames the dataset keeps, once per session, so the frames of a dropped trial do not move the boundaries.\n\nii. The split, and where it is applied:\n```python\ndef quartiles(values):\n    \"\"\"Split into four bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n    return rank * 4 // values.size\n```\n```python\nkept = np.concatenate([frames[trial] for trial in trials])\nspeed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\nspeed[window]\n```\n\niii. Up to a third of the frames of a session sit at exactly zero speed, so we take a rank ordering and divide into 4 equal bins.\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *running_speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. `ft_RunSpeed` gives one speed per imaging frame, so it is already on the same grid as the neural data. It is taken with the same `window` of frames used for the neural columns of that trial, so it has that trial's length.\n\nii. The same frames as the neural data of the trial:\n```python\nneural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\nspeed[window]\n```\n\niii. All data stream is aligned in this dataset based on frame number. \n\n## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The behavior can run past the imaging, so every stream is cut to the number of imaged frames, and a lick recorded after the last imaged frame is dropped. A trial left with no frames is dropped.\n\nii. The cut to the imaged frames, the licks kept, and the failure that does not stop the run:\n```python\nn_frames = spikes.shape[1]\nframe_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\n```\n```python\nlicking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n```\n\niii. The reference code cuts the same way, `beh[...][:nfr]` with `nfr = spk.shape[1]`. Beyond that there is nothing to handle. This is a very clean dataset.\n\n## 12-a. 79→    return category\n    80→\n    81→\n    82→def array_equal_safe(a, b):\n    83→    a_arr = np.asarray(a)\n    84→    b_arr = np.asarray(b)\n    85→    if a_arr.dtype.kind in {\"U\", \"S\", \"O\"} or b_arr.dtype.kind in {\"U\", \"S\", \"O\"}:\n    86→        return np.array_equal(a_arr, b_arr)\n    87→    return np.array_equal(a_arr, b_arr, equal_nan=True)\n    88→\n    89→\n    90→def behavior_sessions_match(a, b):\n    91→    fields = [\n    92→        \"ntrials\",\n    93→        \"UniqWalls\",\n    94→        \"WallName\",\n    95→        \"isRew\",\n    96→        \"SoundPos\",\n    97→        \"SoundDelPos\",\n    98→        \"RewPos\",\n    99→        \"LickPos\",\n   100→        \"LickTrind\",\n   101→        \"ft_trInd\",\n   102→        \"ft_Pos\",\n   103→        \"ft_move\",\n   104→        \"ft_CorrSpc\",\n   105→        \"ft_GraySpc\",\n   106→        \"ft_RunSpeed\",\n   107→    ]\n   108→    for field in fields:\n   109→        if not array_equal_safe(a[field], b[field]):\n   110→            return False, field\n   111→    return True, None\n   112→\n   113→\n   114→def load_canonical_behavior_sessions():\n   115→    exp_info =      for record in records:\n   125→            full_key = full_key_from_record(record)\n   126→            base_key = f\"{record['mname']}_{record['datexp']}_{record['blk']}\"\n   127→ )\n   144→    return canonical\n   145→\n   146→\n   147→def get_spk_session_keys():\n   148→    session_keys = []\n   149→    for filename in sorted(os.listdir(SPK_DIR)):\n   150→        if not 156→def compute_training_day_by_session(session_keys):\n   157→    by_mouse = defaultdict(list)\n   158→    for session_key in session_keys:\n   159→        mouse, _, _ = parse_base_key(session_key)\n   160→        by_mouse[mouse].append(session_key)\n   161→\n   162→    first_day = {}\n   163→    for mouse, keys in by_mouse.items():\n   164→        first_day[mouse] = min(date_from_base_key(key) for key in keys)\n   165→\n   166→    training_day = {}\n   167→    for session_key in session_keys:\n   168→        mouse, _, _ = parse_base_key(session_key)\n   169→        training_day[session_key] = float((date_from_base_key(session_key) - first_day[mouse]).days)\n   170→    return training_day\n   171→\n   172→\n   173→def build_region_index(iarea):\n   174→    region_idx = np.full(len(iarea), -1, dtype=np.int16)\n   175→    region_idx[iarea == 8] = 0\n   176→    region_idx[np.isin(iarea, [0, 1, 2, 9])] = 1\n   177→    region_idx[np.isin(iarea, [5, 6])] = 2\n   178→    region_idx[np.isin(iarea, [3, 4])] = 3\n   179→    return region_idx\n   180→\n   181→\n   182→def sort_take_desc(scores, indices, n_take):\n   183→    if len(indices) <= n_take:\n   184→        return indices\n   185→    order = np.argsort(scores, kind=\"mergesort\")[::-1]\n   186→    return indices[order[:n_take]]\n   187→\n   188→\n   189→def select_visual_neurons(spk_planes, region_idx_full, corridor_mask, gray_mask, max_per_region):\n   190→    responsive_by_region = defaultdict(list)\n   191→    fallback_by_region = defaultdict(list)\n   192→\n   193→    offset = 0\n   194→    for plane_idx, plane in enumerate(spk_planes):\n   195→        n_neurons = plane.shape[0]\n   196→        plane_regions = region_idx_full[offset : offset + n_neurons]\n   197→        valid_local = np.flatnonzero(plane_regions >= 0)\n   198→\n   199→        if len(valid_local):\n   200→            plane_valid = plane[valid_local]\n   201→            corridor_data = plane_valid[:, corridor_mask]\n   202→            corridor_mean = corridor_data.mean(axis=1)\n   203→            corridor_var = corridor_data.var(axis=1)\n   204→            del corridor_data\n   205→\n   206→            if gray_mask.any():\n   207→                gray_mean = plane_valid[:, gray_mask].mean(axis=1)\n   208→                responsive = corridor_mean > gray_mean\n   209→            else:\n   210→                responsive = np.ones_like(corridor_mean, dtype=bool)\n   211→\n   212→            responsive &= np.isfinite(corridor_var)\n   213→\n   214→            for region in range(len(VISUAL_CORTEX_REGIONS)):\n   215→                region_mask = plane_regions[valid_local] == region\n   216→                if not np.any(region_mask):\n   217→                    continue\n   218→\n   219→                region_local = valid_local[region_mask]\n   220→                region_scores = corridor_var[region_mask]\n   221→                region_responsive = responsive[region_mask]\n   222→\n   223→                global_rows = region_local + offset\n   224→                responsive_by_region[region].append(\n   225→                    (global_rows[region_responsive], region_scores[region_responsive])\n   226→                )\n   227→                fallback_by_region[region].append((global_rows, region_scores))\n   228→\n   229→        offset += n_neurons\n   230→\n   231→    chosen = []\n   232→    for region in range(len(VISUAL_CORTEX_REGIONS)):\n   233→        resp_indices = np.concatenate(\n   234→            [chunk[0] for chunk in responsive_by_region[region]] or [np.empty(0, dtype=np.int64)]\n   235→        )\n   236→        resp_scores = np.concatenate(\n   237→            [chunk[1] for chunk in responsive_by_region[region]] or [np.empty(0, dtype=np.float32)]\n   238→        )\n   239→        region_selected = sort_take_desc(resp_scores, resp_indices, max_per_region)\n   240→\n   241→        if len(region_selected) < max_per_region:\n   242→            all_indices = np.concatenate(\n   243→                [chunk[0] for chunk in fallback_by_region[region]] or [np.empty(0, dtype=np.int64)]\n   244→            )\n   245→            all_scores = np.concatenate(\n   246→                [chunk[1] for chunk in fallback_by_region[region]] or [np.empty(0, dtype=np.float32)]\n   247→            )\n   248→            if len(all_indices):\n   249→                already = set(region_selected.tolist())\n   250→                fill_mask = np.array([idx not in already for idx in all_indices], dtype=bool)\n   251→                fill_indices = all_indices[fill_mask]\n   252→                fill_scores = all_scores[fill_mask]\n   253→                n_fill = max_per_region - len(region_selected)\n   254→                fill_selected = sort_take_desc(fill_scores, fill_indices, n_fill)\n   255→                region_selected = np.concatenate([region_selected, fill_selected])\n   256→\n   257→        chosen.append(region_selected)\n   258→\n   259→    if not chosen:\n   260→        return np.empty(0, dtype=np.int64)\n   261→\n   262→    selected_global = np.concatenate(chosen)\n   263→    selected_global = np.unique(selected_global)\n   264→    selected_global.sort()\n   265→    return selected_global.astype(np.int64)\n   266→\n   267→\n   268→def split_global_indices_by_plane(global_indices, plane_sizes):\n   269→    by_plane = []\n   270→    start = 0\n   271→    for size in plane_sizes:\n   272→        end = start + size\n   273→        mask = (global_indices >= start) & (global_indices < end)\n   274→        by_plane.append((global_indices[mask] - start).astype(np.int64))\n   275→        start = end\n   276→    return by_plane\n   277→\n   278→\n   279→def nearest_retained_licks(retained_frame_idx, lick_frame_idx, lick_pos, texture_length):\n   280→    lick_binary = np.zeros(len(retained_frame_idx), dtype=np.int8)\n   281→    if len(retained_frame_idx) == 0:\n   282→        return lick_binary\n   283→\n   284→    lick_frame_idx = np.asarray(lick_frame_idx, dtype=np.float64)\n   285→    lick_pos = np.asarray(lick_pos, dtype=np.float64)\n   286→    valid = np.isfinite(lick_frame_idx) & np.isfinite(lick_pos) & (lick_pos >= 0.0) & (lick_pos < texture_length)\n   287→    lick_frame_idx = lick_frame_idx[valid]\n   288→    if len(lick_frame_idx) == 0:\n   289→        return lick_binary\n   290→\n   291→    retained = retained_frame_idx.astype(np.float64)\n   292→    insert = np.searchsorted(retained, lick_frame_idx)\n   293→    candidate_left = np.clip(insert - 1, 0, len(retained) - 1)\n   294→    candidate_right = np.clip(insert, 0, len(retained) - 1)\n   295→\n   296→    left_dist = np.abs(retained[candidate_left] - lick_frame_idx)\n   297→    right_dist = np.abs(retained[candidate_right] - lick_frame_idx)\n   298→    use_right = right_dist < left_dist\n   299→    nearest = candidate_left.copy()\n   300→    nearest[use_right] = candidate_right[use_right]\n   301→    nearest_dist = np.minimum(left_dist, right_dist)\n   302→\n   303→    lick_binary[np.unique(nearest[nearest_dist <= 0.5])] = 1\n   304→    return lick_binary\n   305→\n   306→\n   307→def make_position_bins(position, texture_length):\n   308→    pos = np.clip(np.asarray(position, dtype=np.float32), 0.0, float(texture_length) - 1e-6)\n   309→    bin_edges = np.linspace(0.0, float(texture_length), 5)\n   310→    return np.clip(np.digitize(pos, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False), 0, 3).astype(np.int8)\n   311→\n   312→\n   313→def make_speed_bins(speed, speed_edges):\n   314→    return np.clip(np.digitize(np.asarray(speed, dtype=np.float32), speed_edges, right=False), 0, 3).astype(np.int8)\n   315→\n   316→\n   317→def compute_speed_edges(session_keys, canonical_behavior):\n   318→    all_speeds = []\n   319→    for session_key in session_keys:\n   320→        sess = canonical_behavior[session_key][\"behavior\"]\n   321→        mask = sess[\"ft_CorrSpc\"] & (sess[\"ft_move\"] > 0)\n   322→        if np.any(mask):\n   323→            all_speeds.append(np.asarray(sess[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][mask], dtype=np.float32))\n   324→    speed_values = np.concatenate(all_speeds)\n   325→    q25, q50, q75 = np.quantile(speed_values, [0.25, 0.5, 0.75])\n   326→    return np.array([q25, q50, q75], dtype=np.float32)\n   327→\n   328→\n   329→def build_subject_metadata(session_keys):\n   330→    subjects = sorted({parse_base_key(key)[0] for build_region_index(retino[\"iarea\"])\n   341→\n   342→\n   343→def build_selected_session_matrix(spk_planes, selected_global):\n   344→    plane_sizes = [plane.shape[0] for plane in spk_planes]\n   345→    per_plane = split_global_indices_by_plane(selected_global, plane_sizes)\n   346→    selected_parts = []\n   347→    for plane, local_idx in zip(spk_planes, per_plane):\n   348→        if speed_edges,\n   360→    max_neurons_per_region,\n   361→):\n   362→    behavior = canonical_behavior_entry[\"behavior\"]\n   363→    spk_path = os.path.join(SPK_DIR, {session_key}\")\n   370→\n   371→    ft_trind = np.asarray(behavior[\"ft_trInd\"][:n_frames])\n   372→    ft_pos = np.asarray(behavior[\"ft_Pos\"][:n_frames], dtype=np.float32)\n   373→    ft_move = np.asarray(behavior[\"ft_move\"][:n_frames], dtype=np.float32)\n   374→    ft_corr = np.asarray(behavior[\"ft_CorrSpc\"][:n_frames], dtype=bool)\n   375→    ft_gray = np.asarray(behavior[\"ft_GraySpc\"][:n_frames], dtype=bool)\n   376→    ft_speed = np.asarray(behavior[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][:n_frames], dtype=np.float32)\n   377→    ft_time = np.asarray(behavior[\"ft\"][:n_frames], dtype=np.float64)\n   378→\n   379→    retained_mask = ft_corr & (ft_move > 0)\n   380→    gray_running_mask = ft_gray & (ft_move > 0)\n   381→\n   382→ selected_global)\n   394→    del spk_planes\n   395→    gc.collect()\n   396→\n   397→    texture_length = float(behavior.get(\"Texture_Length\", 40.0))\n   398→    n_trials = int(behavior[\"ntrials\"])\n   399→\n   400→    neural_trials = []\n   401→    input_trials = []\n   402→    output_trials = []\n   403→\n   404→    trial_start_time = np.asarray(behavior[\"Trial_start_time\"], dtype=np.float64)\n   405→    sound_time = np.asarray(behavior[\"SoundTime\"], dtype=np.float64)\n   406→    lick_trial_index = np.asarray(behavior[\"LickTrind\"], dtype=np.float64)\n   407→    lick_frame_idx = np.asarray(behavior[\"LickFr\"], dtype=np.float64)\n   408→    lick_pos = np.asarray(behavior[\"LickPos\"], dtype=np.float64)\n   409→    wall_names = np.asarray(behavior[\"WallName\"])\n   410→    is_rew = np.asarray(behavior[\"isRew\"], dtype=bool)\n   411→\n   412→    for trial in range(n_trials):\n   413→        frame_idx = np.flatnonzero((ft_trind == trial) & retained_mask)\n   414→        if len(frame_idx) == 0:\n   415→            continue\n   416→\n   417→        neural = session_matrix[:, frame_idx].astype(np.float16, copy=False)\n   418→\n   419→        times_since_start = ((ft_time[frame_idx] - trial_start_time[trial]) * 24.0 * 3600.0).astype(np.float32)\n   420→        times_to_sound = ((sound_time[trial] - ft_time[frame_idx]) * 24.0 * 3600.0).astype(np.float32)\n   421→        reward_available = np.full(len(frame_idx), float(is_rew[trial]), dtype=np.float32)\n   422→        training_day = np.full(len(frame_idx), day_of_training, dtype=np.float32)\n   423→\n   424→        input_arr = np.vstack(\n   425→            [\n   426→                times_to_sound,\n   433→        trial_lick_mask = lick_trial_index == trial\n   434→        licking = nearest_retained_licks(\n   435→            retained_frame_idx=frame_idx,\n   436→            lick_frame_idx=lick_frame_idx[trial_lick_mask],\n   437→            lick_pos=lick_pos[trial_lick_mask],\n   438→            texture_length=texture_length,\n   439→        )\n   440→\n   441→ make_position_bins(ft_pos[frame_idx], texture_length)\n   444→        speed_out = make_speed_bins(ft_speed[frame_idx], speed_edges)\n   445→\n   446→        output_arr = np.vstack(\n   447→            [\n   448→                stim_out,\n   449→                licking,\n   450→                position_out,\n   451→                speed_out,\n   452→            ]\n   453→        ).astype(np.int8,     \"input\": input_trials,\n   468→        \"output\": output_trials,\n   469→        \"brain_region_idx\": selected_brain_regions,\n   470→        \"session_info\": {\n   471→        \"day_of_training\": day_of_training,\n   475→            \"n_trials_retained\": len(neural_trials),\n   476→            \"n_neurons_retained\": int(len(selected_brain_regions)),\n   477→        },\n   478→    }\n   479→\n   480→\n   481→def convert_dataset(output_path, max_neurons_per_region, limit_sessions=None):\n   482→    canonical_behavior = load_canonical_behavior_sessions()\n   483→    session_keys = get_spk_session_keys()\n   484→    if limit_sessions is not None:\n   485→        session_keys = session_keys[:limit_sessions]\n   486→\n   487→    missing_behavior = [key for key in  \"time_to_sound_cue_s\",\n   505→            \"day_of_training\",\n   506→            \"time_since_trial_start_s\",\n   507→            \"reward_available\",\n   508→        ],\n   509→ ],\n   515→        \"output_values\": [\n   516→            STIM_CATEGORY_VALUES,\n   517→            [\"no_lick\", \"lick\"],\n   518→            [\"0-1m\", \"1-2m\", \"2-3m\", \"3-4m\"],\n   519→            [\"speed_q1\", \"speed_q2\", \"speed_q3\", \"speed_q4\"],\n   520→        ],\n   521→        \"metadata\": {\n   522→            \"task_description\": (\n   523→                \"Head-fixed         },\n   542→            \"running_speed_bin_edges_cm_s\": speed_edges.astype(float).tolist(),\n   543→            \"day_of_training_definition\": \"Calendar days since the first imaging session ID: d93bfe\\nWall time: 0.0000 seconds\\nProcess exited with code 0\\nOriginal token count: 2346\\nOutput:\\nSimilar to previous work, we designed a visual discrimination task in head-fixed mice running through linear virtual reality corridors (Fig. 1a). Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as \\u2018frozen\\u2019 crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as \\u2018leaf\\u2019 and \\u2018circle\\u2019, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (\\u2018rock\\u2019 and \\u2018bricks\\u2019; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli \\u2018leaf2\\u2019 and \\u2018circle2\\u2019, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (\\u2018leaf3\\u2019) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\\n\\nMice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\\n\\nBefore and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\\n\\nIt is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \\n\\nMethods\\n\\nWe performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s \\u00d7 camK2a-tTa mice.\\n\\nThe mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\\n\\nVisual stimuli\\n\\nWe showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270\\u00b0 of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4\\u2009m long, with 2\\u2009m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\\n\\nThe virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0\\u00b0 and 45\\u00b0) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (\\u2018unsupervised gratings\\u2019 and VRg mice).\\n\\nFor behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\\n\\nFor imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\\n\\nThe two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\\n\\nBehavioural training\\n\\nAll animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5\\u20131\\u2009h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1\\u2009h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality\\u2009+\\u2009reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\\n\\nFor the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality\\u2009+\\u2009reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2\\u2009m and 3\\u2009m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2\\u20133\\u2009m.\\n\\nFor imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5\\u2009m and 3.5\\u2009m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5\\u2009m and 3.5\\u2009m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\\n\\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\\n\\nCalcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Suite2p performs motion correction, region of interest detection, cell classification, neuropil correction and spike deconvolution as previously described64. For non-negative deconvolution, we used a timescale of decay of 0.75\\u2009s (ref. 65). A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\\n\\n## Reference Information\\n\\n- The reference **paper** \\\"Unsupervised pretraining in biological neural networks\\\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \\n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\\n\\n## Decoder Task\\n\\nTemporally aligned based on trial start (corridor entry).\\n\\n### Decoder Inputs:\\n- Time to sound cue, continuous, time-varying\\n- Day of training, continuous, per-trial\\n- Time since trial start, continuous, time varying\\n- Reward availability: 1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not, discrete, per-trial\\n\\n### Decoder Outputs\\n- Visual stimulus category. e.g. circle, leaf, etc., per-trial\\n- Licking, binary, time-varying. 0 = not licking, 1 = licking\\n- Position in corridor discretized into 4 equal-length, 1-m-long spatial bins, time varying\\n- Running speed discretized into 4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data\\n\\n## Target Data Format\\n\\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\\n\\n```python\\ndata = {\\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\\n\\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\\n\\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'metadata': {\\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        ...\\n    }\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Key Format Requirements\\n\\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \\n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\\n\\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \\n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\\n\\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\\n\\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \\n\\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\\n\\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \\n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\\n\\n### Consistency requirements\\n\\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\\n- Loading of data\\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\\n\\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\\n\\n## Success Criteria\\n\\nA successful conversion should:\\n1. All required files must be created:\\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\\n  - Decoder accuracy\\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.
