designed a visual discrimination task in head-fixed mice running through linear virtual reality corridors (Fig. 1a). Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as \\u2018frozen\\u2019 crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as \\u2018leaf\\u2019 and \\u2018circle\\u2019, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (\\u2018rock\\u2019 and \\u2018bricks\\u2019; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli \\u2018leaf2\\u2019 and \\u2018circle2\\u2019, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (\\u2018leaf3\\u2019) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\\n\\nMice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\\n\\nBefore and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\\n\\nIt is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \\n\\nMethods\\n\\nWe performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s \\u00d7 camK2a-tTa mice.\\n\\nThe mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\\n\\nVisual stimuli\\n\\nWe showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270\\u00b0 of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4\\u2009m long, with 2\\u2009m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\\n\\nThe virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0\\u00b0 and 45\\u00b0) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (\\u2018unsupervised gratings\\u2019 and VRg mice).\\n\\nFor behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\\n\\nFor imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\\n\\nThe two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\\n\\nBehavioural training\\n\\nAll animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5\\u20131\\u2009h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1\\u2009h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality\\u2009+\\u2009reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\\n\\nFor the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality\\u2009+\\u2009reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2\\u2009m and 3\\u2009m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2\\u20133\\u2009m.\\n\\nFor imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5\\u2009m and 3.5\\u2009m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5\\u2009m and 3.5\\u2009m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as \\u2018frozen\\u2019 crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as \\u2018leaf\\u2019 and \\u2018circle\\u2019, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (\\u2018rock\\u2019 and \\u2018bricks\\u2019; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli \\u2018leaf2\\u2019 and \\u2018circle2\\u2019, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (\\u2018leaf3\\u2019) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\\n\\nMice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\\n\\nBefore and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\\n\\nIt is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \\n\\nMethods\\n\\nWe performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s \\u00d7 camK2a-tTa mice.\\n\\nThe mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\\n\\nVisual stimuli\\n\\nWe showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270\\u00b0 of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4\\u2009m long, with 2\\u2009m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\\n\\nThe virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0\\u00b0 and 45\\u00b0) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (\\u2018unsupervised gratings\\u2019 and VRg mice).\\n\\nFor behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\\n\\nFor imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf\\u2013circle or rock\\u2013brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\\n\\nThe two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\\n\\nBehavioural training\\n\\nAll animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5\\u20131\\u2009h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1\\u2009h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality\\u2009+\\u2009reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\\n\\nFor the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality\\u2009+\\u2009reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2\\u2009m and 3\\u2009m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2\\u20133\\u2009m.\\n\\nFor imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5\\u2009m and 3.5\\u2009m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5\\u2009m and 3.5\\u2009m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\\n\\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\\n\\nCalcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Same source as the reference.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position in corridor*?\n\ni. Position is clipped to `[0, texture_length)` and then binned into 4 equal bins using `np.digitize` with edges at `[0, 10, 20, 30, 40]` (for texture_length=40 dm). This gives 4 bins of 10 dm = 1 m each.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef make_position_bins(position, texture_length):\n    pos = np.clip(np.asarray(position, dtype=np.float32), 0.0, float(texture_length) - 1e-6)\n    bin_edges = np.linspace(0.0, float(texture_length), 5)\n    return np.clip(np.digitize(pos, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False), 0, 3).astype(np.int8)\n```\n\niii. Uses `np.digitize` instead of integer division, but produces the same 4 x 1m bins.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Four equal-length 1m bins: 0-1m, 1-2m, 2-3m, 3-4m. Same as reference.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_edges = np.linspace(0.0, float(texture_length), 5)  # [0, 10, 20, 30, 40]\n```\n\niii. Same source as the reference.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. The AI computes global speed edges from the 25th, 50th, and 75th percentiles of running speeds across ALL sessions, using only running corridor frames (`ft_CorrSpc & ft_move > 0`). It then uses `np.digitize` to bin per-trial speeds. This differs from the reference which uses per-session rank-based quartiles.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_speed_edges(session_keys, canonical_behavior):\n    all_speeds = []\n    for session_key in session_keys:\n        sess = canonical_behavior[session_key][\"behavior\"]\n        mask = sess[\"ft_CorrSpc\"] & (sess[\"ft_move\"] > 0)\n        if np.any(mask):\n            all_speeds.append(np.asarray(sess[\"ft_RunSpeed\"][mask], dtype=np.float32))\n    speed_values = np.concatenate(all_speeds)\n    q25, q50, q75 = np.quantile(speed_values, [0.25, 0.5, 0.75])\n    return np.array([q25, q50, q75], dtype=np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The agent uses global quantile-based edges applied uniformly, while the reference uses per-session rank-based splits.\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Four bins using global percentile edges (25th, 50th, 75th percentile of all running speeds). Each frame's speed is digitized into one of 4 bins.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef make_speed_bins(speed, speed_edges):\n    return np.clip(np.digitize(np.asarray(speed, dtype=np.float32), speed_edges, right=False), 0, 3).astype(np.int8)\n```\n\niii. a session id sorts by date within a mouse, so the sessions are counted in order\n    for subject, session_id, _, _ in sorted(records):\n        days[session_id] = count.get(subject, 0)\n        count[subject] = days[session_id] + 1\n\n    return days\n\n\n# --- trials ---\n\ndef trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames):\n    \"\"\"Neural frames of one trial, from corridor entry to the grey space.\"\"\"\n    # the authors label every frame with its trial and whether it is inside the texture\n    inside = (beh['ft_trInd'][:n_frames] == trial) & beh['ft_CorrSpc'][:n_frames]\n    return np.flatnonzero(inside)\n\n\ndef get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Compute the length of every trial in the dataset, in imaging frames.\n\n    Collect all trial lengths based on behavior data (approximately matches neural data)\n\n    records: list of (subject, session_id, beh_file, beh_key), every session of the dataset.\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n\n    Returns an int array of per trial frame counts, without the trials that were never imaged.\n    \"\"\"\n    # a behavior file holds several sessions, so it is read once for all of them\n    groups = {}\n    for record in records:\n        groups.setdefault(record[2], []).append(record)\n\n    lengths = []\n    for beh_file, group in groups.items():\n        behavior = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'beh', beh_file), allow_pickle=True).item()\n        for _, _, _, beh_key in group:\n            beh = behavior[beh_key]\n            n_frames = beh['ft_trInd'].size          # the behavior grid, so no spike file needed\n            for trial in range(beh['ntrials']):\n                size = trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames).size\n                if size:\n                    lengths.append(size)\n        del behavior\n\n    return np.array(lengths)\n\n\ndef get_max_trial_length(records, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Compute longest trial to keep, as a percentile over every trial in the dataset.\n\n    Measured over the whole dataset even when only a sample is converted, so that a sample and a\n    full run agree on which trials to keep.\n\n    records: list of (subject, session_id, beh_file, beh_key), every session of the dataset.\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n\n    Returns the limit in frames as a float, and the lengths it was measured from.\n    \"\"\"\n    lengths = get_all_trial_lengths(records, datadir)\n    return np.percentile(lengths, TRIAL_LENGTH_PERCENTILE), lengths\n\n\ndef quartiles(values):\n    \"\"\"Split into four bins of a quarter of the data each.\"\"\"\n    # a quarter or more of the frames can sit at exactly zero speed, and no threshold can\n    # cut a tie like that into equal parts, so the split is on rank rather than on value\n    rank = np.empty(values.size, dtype=int)\n    rank[np.argsort(values, kind='stable')] = np.arange(values.size)\n    return rank * 4 // values.size\n\n\n# --- neural ---\n\ndef load_spikes(session_id, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Deconvolved traces of the neurons of one session, and the visual area of each.\n\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n    \"\"\"\n    # the traces come one imaging plane at a time, in the order the retinotopy indexes them\n    planes = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'spk', '%s_neural_data.npy' % session_id),\n                     allow_pickle=True).item()\n    spikes = np.concatenate(planes['spks'], 0)\n\n    mouse, year, month, day, _ = session_id.split('_')\n    area = np.load(os.path.join(datadir, 'retinotopy',\n                                '%s_%s_%s_%s_trans.npz' % (mouse, year, month, day)))['iarea']\n\n    # a neuron outside the four areas, or with no area at all, is dropped\n    region = np.full(area.size, -1)\n    for index, name in enumerate(BRAIN_REGIONS):\n        region[np.isin(area, AREAS[name])] = index\n\n    keep = region >= 0\n    return spikes[keep], region[keep]\n\n\n# --- session ---\n\ndef process_session(subject, session_id, beh, day, datadir, max_trial_length):\n    \"\"\"One session as lists of per trial neural, input and output arrays.\n\n    datadir: directory holding the beh/, spk/ and retinotopy/ subfolders.\n    max_trial_length: longest trial to keep, in frames, from get_max_trial_length.\n    \"\"\"\n    spikes, region = load_spikes(session_id, datadir)\n\n    # the behavior can run past the imaging, so every stream is cut to the frames that were imaged\n    n_frames = spikes.shape[1]\n    frame_time = (beh['ft'][:n_frames] - beh['ft'][0]) * SEC_PER_DAY\n\n    # a trial whose corridor was not imaged has no frames left, and one longer than the limit is\n    # an animal that stopped rather than one that ran slowly, so neither is a traversal\n    frames = [trial_frames(beh, trial, n_frames) for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])]\n    trials = [trial for trial in range(beh['ntrials'])\n              if 0 < frames[trial].size <= max_trial_length]\n    if not trials:\n        return {'session_id': session_id, 'subject': subject, 'day': day,\n                'neural': [], 'input': [], 'output': [],\n                'n_trials': 0, 'n_units': region.size, 'regions': region}\n    kept = np.concatenate([frames[trial] for trial in trials])\n\n    # licking comes as the frame number of each lick, so make it a flag per frame\n    licking = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=int)\n    lick_frame = beh['LickFr'].astype(int)\n    licking[lick_frame[lick_frame < n_frames]] = 1\n\n    # position bins are 10 decimeters, the 1 m the task asks for\n    position = np.clip(beh['ft_Pos'][:n_frames] // 10, 0, 3).astype(int)\n\n    # the quartiles are over the frames the dataset keeps, so each bin holds a quarter of it\n    speed = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=int)\n    speed[kept] = quartiles(beh['ft_RunSpeed'][kept])\n\n    stimulus = np.array([STIMULUS.index(TEXTURE[name]) for name in beh['WallName']])\n    reward = beh['isRew'].astype(int)\n\n    # both events fall between frames, so they are interpolated onto the time axis\n    index = np.arange(n_frames)\n    start = np.interp(beh['StartFr'], index, frame_time)\n    cue = np.interp(beh['SoundFr'], index, frame_time)\n\n    neural, inputs, outputs = [], [], []\n    for trial in trials:\n        window = frames[trial]\n        n_frames = window.size\n        time = frame_time[window]\n\n        neural.append(spikes[:, window].astype(np.float16))\n        inputs.append(np.stack([cue[trial] - time,\n                                np.full(n_frames, day),\n                                time - start[trial],\n                                np.full(n_frames, reward[trial])]).astype(np.float32))\n        outputs.append(np.stack([np.full(n_frames, stimulus[trial]),\n                                 licking[window],\n                                 position[window],\n                                 speed[window]]).astype(np.int8))\n\n    return {'session_id': session_id, 'subject': subject, 'day': day,\n            'neural': neural, 'input': inputs, 'output': outputs,\n            'n_trials': len(neural), 'n_units': region.size, 'regions': region}\n\n\ndef assemble(results):\n    \"\"\"Stitch per-session results into the target dictionary.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n\n    return {\n        'neural': [r['neural'] for r in results],\n        'input': [r['input'] for r in results],\n        'output': [r['output'] for r in results],\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

responsive_by_region[region].append(\n                    (global_rows[region_responsive], region_scores[region_responsive])\n                )\n                fallback_by_region[region].append((global_rows, region_scores))\n\n        offset += n_neurons\n\n    chosen = []\n    for region in range(len(VISUAL_CORTEX_REGIONS)):\n        resp_indices = Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as ‘frozen’ crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as ‘leaf’ and ‘circle’, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (‘rock’ and ‘bricks’; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli ‘leaf2’ and ‘circle2’, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs.
